2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 聯準會上周三決議升息一碼至 3.75%-4.00%區間，FOMC 政策聲明顯示，18 名
FOMC 成員中有 12 人預計今年將再升息一次 ， 另有4 人認為存在升息兩次的可
能性 ，加上聯準會主席華許(Kevin Warsh)會後表達控制通膨的偏鷹立場 ， 帶動美
元指數走升至 100 高位。
⚫ 歐元區 9 月 ZEW 經濟景氣指數自前月的 31.4 降至 25.8，低於預估的 39.9。數
據下滑顯示在美伊衝突未獲緩解 、 能源價格持續高位情況下 ， 市場對於歐元區經
濟前景抱持較不樂觀的態度。
⚫ 美國公布初請失業金人數為 19.6 萬人，優於市場預期的 20.8 萬，再度呼應聯準
會主席華許(Kevin Warsh)認為目前勞動力市場仍具韌性的說法， 在美國經濟穩
健及通膨頑固的情況下，預計聯準會將持續升息，有利美元持穩。
⚫ 歐洲央行管委暨葡萄牙央行總裁佩雷拉(Alvaro Santos Pereira)表示，歐元區目前

通膨雖未廣泛擴散 ， 惟考量近期天然氣價格飆升 ， 央行將密切關注未來通膨壓力
擴散情形。最新利率期貨市場顯示，歐洲央行今年底仍有再升息一碼空間。
⚫ 日本政府通過消費稅減稅及家庭現金補貼的政策大綱，預計自明年 4 月起的兩
年內，將食品消費稅率自 8%降至 1%，惟大綱中未明確說明資金來源，再度引
發市場對於日本政府的財政擔憂，短線不利日圓表現。
⚫ 台灣央行 9/17 宣布重貼現率連 10 凍維持在 2%不變，央行總裁楊金龍表示，台
灣的通膨相較其他主要經濟體溫和，採取漸進緊縮貨幣政策有助物價穩定。 此
外，目前通膨率為 2%，明年有望降至 2%以下，後續將觀察通膨發展、主要經
濟體貨幣政策緊縮程度及台灣金融情勢，以決定未來貨幣政策走向。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.650，低點在 31.958，以 31.808 作收，較前一周收盤價
升值 1 角 7 分。 
上週台幣震盪走貶 ， 美國CPI 數據公布後加強市場對於聯準會 9 月升息的預期 ，
早盤台幣跳空開低，外資連續三日賣超台股並匯出，加以出口商態度轉為惜售，令
台幣接連貶破 31.700、31.800、31.900 關卡， 週四由於聯準會偏鷹派升息，美元指
數走升至 100.300，早盤台幣跳空開低，台幣最低貶至31.958，不過隨後出口商賣壓
出籠，外資雙向進出，台幣收復部分跌幅，維持在31.900 附近震盪；臨近周末，外
資買超台股並於外匯市場匯入 ， 台幣走升至31.722， 最終在央行調節之下收在31.808。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週台幣區間震盪 。 就國際市場而言 ， 美國最新初領失業救濟金人數較預
期減少一萬多人、續領失業救濟金人數亦下降，顯示美國勞動力市場尚屬穩健，加
以聯準會升息抗通膨，鷹派立場給予美元指數支撐、不利台幣表現；就台灣市場來
看，九月台灣央行並未升息，台美利差擴大預計將帶給台幣壓力，此外，儘管聯準
會升息利空出盡，外資買超台股帶動台幣走升，惟台灣即將迎來中秋連假，預計金
融市場交投謹慎，將令台幣陷入橫盤整理格局，綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間
震盪。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/21/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪走升。人民幣中間價連續走升，接連升破 6.7700、6.7600 整數
關口，升至三年七個月來高點，帶動人民幣偏升，不過由於美元指數震盪走強，加
以市場靜待聯準會利率決議公布 ， 交投謹慎 ， 人民幣升幅有限 ， 大致維持高檔震盪 ，
週四聯準會鷹派升息一碼，惟人民幣中間價連續五日走升至 6.7521，並創下四年高
點，帶動人民幣緩步走升，USD/CNH 升破 6.7000 整數大關，終場境外人民幣收在
6.6944，週線升值 94 點，境內人民幣則收在 6.6984，週線升值 21 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣震盪走升 。 就國際市場來看 ，FED 最新政策聲明和經濟預測顯
示，聯準會為明年繼續收緊貨幣政策敞開了大門，美元指數走升，抑制人民幣升值
的速度；就中國國內市場來看，中間價持續偏升將帶動人民幣走升，加以出口商實
質拋匯需求將支撐人民幣表現； 最後就技術面來看 ，USD/CNH 日線圖中 ， 匯價維持
在五日均線之下交投，均線呈現空頭排列，加以USD/CNH 有效跌破 6.7000 整數關
卡，顯示 USD/CNH 空方勢力較盛，綜合以上，預估本週人民幣震盪走升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6700-6.7200

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週日圓走貶。 週初，美國通膨數據維持高位 ，令聯準會升息預期升溫 ，USD/JPY
自 153.29 低點反彈至 155 附近；週間 ，美國聯準會宣布升息一碼 ， 帶動USD/JPY 上
漲至 156.41；臨近週末，日本央行如市場預期升息一碼，惟央行偏鴿派立場令日圓
續貶，帶動 USD/JPY 一度最高來到 158.05，而隨後在日本政府於外匯市場進行匯率
詢價下，USD/JPY 回吐部分漲幅，終場收在 156.86，週線上漲 349 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、日本政府上周通過消費稅減
稅及家庭現金補貼政策大綱，惟大綱中未明確說明財源，再度引發市場對於日本政
府財政惡化的擔憂，預計將不利日圓走勢。二、日本央行上周五決議將政策利率調
升一碼至 1.25%， 9 名決策委員中有 2 人對升息持反對意見，降低市場對日本央行
進一步升息的預期。另一方面 ， 聯準會上周三亦升息一碼至3.75%-4.00%區間 ，FOMC
政策聲明顯示，18 位官員中有 12 人預計今年將再升息一次，另有 4 人認為存在升
息兩次的可能性，聯準會升息立場明顯較日本央行更為鷹派，預計將支撐USD/JPY
走勢。三、日本財務大臣片山皋月近期再度重申，將於必要時採取外匯干預行動，
以維護外匯市場有序運行。四、就技術面而言，在日線圖中，USD/JPY 短中天期均
線向下發散走勢偏空，惟近期匯價在多次向下測試 154 均拉回，明顯下方有買盤支
撐。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY 本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：154.80-159.50 
前週交易區間 
153.29 158.05

### Page 6

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Sep/21/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，聯準會升息預期升溫加上歐元區 9 月經濟景氣指
數下滑，令歐元走跌至 1.1525；週間，美國聯準會鷹派升息立場帶動美元走升，歐
元最低來到 1.1460；臨近週末，歐元持續偏弱整理，終場收在 1.1483，週線下跌 107
點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、 歐洲央行管委卡薩克斯
(Martins Kazaks) 表示 ， 為避免美伊戰爭引發的燃料成本上漲開始傳導至工資和其他
商品價格，歐洲央行可能需要漸進式調升利率以抑制通膨。最新利率期貨市場顯示
歐洲央行今年仍有升息一碼的空間，預計將支撐歐元走勢。二、美國聯準會決議將
利率調升一碼至 3.75%-4.00%區間，聯準會主席華許會後偏鷹言論帶動美元指數走
升至 100 上方，預計將限縮歐元走升空間。三、歐元區 9 月 ZEW 經濟景氣指數自
前月的 31.4 降至 25.8，低於預估的 39.9。數據下滑顯示在美伊衝突未獲緩解、能源
價格持續高位情況下，市場對於歐元區經濟前景抱持較不樂觀的態度，將不利歐元
表現。四、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD 近期回測年線未能有效突破後明顯
拉回轉為下行走勢，目前匯價承壓於各天期均線下方，空方動能較佔優勢。綜合以
上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。

＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1350-1.1590 
前週交易區間 
1.1450 1.1599

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                           新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.14-115.09.18 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.67% ~ 1.70% 
公債－10 年期-A15106 1.92 % ~ 1.921% 
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.86% ~ 1.861%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分 ， 本週央行理監事會議決議結果 ， 維持政策利率不變 ， 符合
市場預期 ， 且近兩週央行持續減發定存單 ， 令市場短錢充裕 ， 惟週五銀行間分
配略為不均，部分銀行有資金需求，因此短率持穩於近期區間。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.67%~1.70%，利率高位較上週上升 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週市場交投清淡，5 年期指標券 A15108 週三等殖系統成交在 1.86%，10 年
期指標券 A15106 則未成交，最近成交利率為 1.92~1.921%。 
台灣央行理監事會議如市場預期維持政策利率不變 ， 總裁楊金龍表示 ， 今年國
內通膨仍屬可控，明年有望回落至 2%以下，市場對今年升息疑慮有所降溫，
對台債形成支撐 ， 惟國際債市走勢仍偏空 ， 預期台債殖利率短線仍呈高檔區間
盤整格局，後續關注下週三財政部公布的第四季公債發行計劃，預估 5 年券
區間為 1.79%~1.83%，10 年券區間為 1.90%~1.95%。

## Document 072: 2330台積電 群益

Source PDF: all report pdf/2330台積電 群益.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-04
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

產業速報  
                                        2026 年 9 月 9 日

1 群益投顧 
ASML (ASML US) 與台積電 (2330 TT) 共同推動 High NA EUV 將自現行的 6 吋光罩，
轉移至更大的 12 吋 光 罩。 目標於 2031 年之前建立 12 吋 光 罩 試 產 線(pilot line) ， 並 於
2033 年之前實現微影系統全面就緒。台積電並表示自 2030 年 起 ， 將 在 先 進 製 程 量 產
中導入 ASML 的 High NA  EUV 。台積電與 ASML 共同發起 6 吋光罩轉移至 12 吋光
罩 的 規 格 轉 移 ， 其 戰 略 意 義 在 於 ， 製 訂 規 格 和 標 準 ， 確 保 台 積 電 技 術 領 先 地 位 。 因 此
對 台 積 電 的 投 資 評 等 Buy ，目標價 3,200 元。

研 究 員 ： 陳 俐 妍   
ly.c@capital.com.tw

ASML 與 台 積 電 共 同 推 動 High NA EUV 將 自 現 行 的 6 吋 光 罩 ， 轉 移 至 更 大
的 12 吋 光 罩 ：  
09/07/2026 ASML 與 台 積 電 於 BACUS 國 際 光 學 工 程 學 會(SPIE) 先 進 微 影 技
術 研 討 會 前 宣 布 發 起 產 業 合 作 ， 共 同 推 動High NA EUV 將 自 現 行 的 6 吋光
罩 ， 轉 移 至 更 大 的 12 吋 光 罩 ， 預 期 將 進 一 步 提 升 晶 圓 廠 的 生 產 力 、 降 低 晶
片 製 造 成 本 ， 並 消 除 需 要 2 張 6 吋 光 罩 在 High NA EUV 曝 光 時 進 行 拼 接 的
限 制，使 整 個 半 導 體 產 業 受 惠。此 轉 移 能 讓 先 進 晶 片 製 造 服 務 提 供 者 充 分 利
用 High NA EUV 的 優 勢 ， 更 提 升 未 來 世 代 先 進 晶 片 的 成 本 效 益 。 
目標於 2031 年 之 前 建 立 12 吋 光 罩 試 產 線(pilot line) ，並於 2033 年 之 前 實
現 微 影 系 統 全 面 就 緒 ， 以 支 援 先 進 製 程 量 產 。 台 積 電 並 表 示 自2030 年 起 ，
將 在 先 進 製 程 量 產 中 導 入 ASML 的 High NA 技 術 。  
12 吋光罩 使 High NA 效 率 提 升 ：  
現行 EUV 設備的 NA(Numerical Aperture ， NA) 為 0.33 ， High NA 則 提 高 到
0.55 。 NA 數 值 越 高 ， 解 析 度 也 越 高 、 曝 光 時 能 呈 現 的 細 節 愈 多 。ASML 資
料顯示 High NA 可 將 最 小 解 析 尺 寸 從 現 行 EUV 的約 13 奈 米 進 一 步 縮 小 到
8 奈 米 ， 也 就 是 能 在 晶 圓 上 印 出 更 細 微 的 電 路 圖 形 。 部 分 原 本 需 要2 次 甚 至
更多次曝光才能完成的圖形，也有機會改以單次曝光處理，降低製程複雜
度 、 缺 陷 風 險 與 生 產 周 期 。 
舉 例 來 說 ，0.33 NA EUV 機 台 ， 至 少 需 要 4 張 光 罩 、 分 多 次 曝 光 才 能 完 成
A14 、 A10 金 屬 層 圖 形 ；High NA EUV ， 只 要 1 張 光 罩 就 能 完 成 相 同 圖 形 。
但 解 析 度 提 高 ，High NA 的 光 學 設 計 讓 單 次 可 曝 光 範 圍 縮 小 到 原 本EUV 的
50% ， 製 作 大 顆 晶 片 時 可 能 必 須 分 成2 次 曝 光 ， 再 把 圖 形 拼 接 起 來 。 這 也 是
台積電與 ASML 此 次 推 動 12 吋 光 罩 的 原 因 ， 相 較 現 行 6 吋 光 罩 ， 放 大 光 罩
尺 寸 預 期 能 進 一 步 提 升 晶 圓 廠 生 產 力、降 低 晶 片 製 造 成 本，並 消 除 High NA
曝 光 時 的 拼 接 限 制 。 
台 積 電 預 計 2030 年 開 始 使 用 High NA  EUV ， 推估 High NA 將 會 在 A10 或
A10 以 下 的 先 進 製 程 採 用：  
台積電預計 2030 年 開 始 導入 High NA  EUV ，比 12 吋 光 罩 微 影 系 統 預 計 全
面就緒的 2033 年 更 早 。 這 代 表 台 積 電 初 期 導 入High NA 時 ， 仍 會 先 使 用 現
行 6 吋 光 罩 ， 之 後 再 隨 12 吋 光 罩 與 相 關 系 統 成 熟 逐 步 轉 換 。

2 群益投顧 
根 據 台 積 電 目 前 公 開 的 製 程 藍 圖 ，A13 與 A12 製 程 都 預 計 2029 年 量 產 。 其
中 A13 是 A14 平 台 的 延 伸 ，A12 則 將 超 級 電 軌(Super Power Rail) 背面供電
技術導入 A14 平 台 。 因 此 N2 、 A16 、 A14 、 A13 與 A12 都 早 於 台 積 電 預 計
2030 年 導 入 High NA ， 推 估 High NA 將 會 在 A10 或 A10 以 下 的 先 進 製 程 採
用。  
Intel (INTC US) 和 Samsung (005930 KS) 領 先 台 積 電 採 用 High NA EUV ，但
也 同 時 參 與 12 吋 光 罩 開 發 ：  
Intel 在 2024 年 宣 布 完 成 全 球 首 套 商 用 High NA EUV 設 備 的 組 裝 ；2026 年
High NA 已 經 正 式 進 入 量 產 ， 並 用 於 部 分 18A 製 程 層 。 是 目 前 High NA 導
入 進 度 最 快 的 晶 片 製 造 商 。Samsung 則預計 2028 年導入 DRAM 量 產 ； 台 積
電選擇在 2030 年 導 入 。Intel 和 Samsung 也 同 時 表 示 與 ASML 及 供 應 鏈 合
作 ， 推 動 未 來 轉 向 大 尺 寸 光 罩 。 
台 積 電 與 ASML 共 同 發 起 6 吋 光 罩 轉 移 至 12 吋 光 罩 的 規 格 轉 移，將 領 導 業
界 規 格 ：  
6 吋 光 罩 轉 移 至 12 吋光罩 的 這 項 升 級 非 單 純 的 尺 寸 放 大 ， 而 是 面 臨 生 態 系
與 物 理 極 限 的 多 重 挑 戰 。 整 體 供 應 鏈 重 構 ， 包 括 基 板 與 材 料 以 及 設 備 等 等 。
12 吋 光 罩 石 英 基 板 的 平 坦 度 、 缺 陷 密 度 與 熱 膨 脹 係 數 控 制 難 度 倍 增 。 製 程
設 備 如 薄 膜 沉 積、蝕 刻、清 洗 與 自 動 化 搬 運 設 備 皆 需 重 新 開 發。後 續 檢 測 與
量測極限檢測難度也增加，更大面積的圖形缺陷檢測（ Inspection ）與修補
技 術 需 要 突 破 。 在 12 吋 面 積 上 維 持 奈 米 級 的 對 位 精 度 與 線 寬 均 勻 性 極 為 困
難   
電 子 設 計 自 動 化 (EDA) 工 具 必 須 重 新 適 應 大 尺 寸 曝 光 視 場 與 資 料 處 理 。 12 
吋 光 罩 的 圖 形 檔 案 與 運 算 複 雜 度 將 大 幅 上 升 。 
台積電與 ASML 共 同 發 起 6 吋 光 罩 轉 移 至 12 吋 光 罩 的 規 格 轉 移，其 戰略意
義在於 ，台 積 電 為 全 球 最 大 晶 圓 代 工 廠，為 先 進 製 程 最 大 的 供 應 商，可 以 領
導 光 罩 基 底 供 應 商 、 光 罩 檢 測 設 備 商， 甚 至 EDA 廠 等 整 個 生 態 系 ，除 共同
分攤 ， 跨 越 升 級 12 吋 光 罩 的 龐 大 研 發 成 本外 ， 製 訂 規 格 和 標 準 ， 確 保 台 積
電 技 術 領 先 地 位 。因 此 對 台 積 電 的投 資 評 等 Buy ， 目 標 價 3,200 元。

  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%

  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15%

無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

Increasing power consumption, along with the deployment of
BBU/CBU/800/±400VDC/SST  in AI clusters, could push the
technology barrier higher and present growth opportunities for
players with superior capabilities. Fuel cells could also emerge

as a new opportunity amid power constraints.
Secular demand for power amid AI capex: AI-related power consumption will
continue to increase, and we believe this will lead to secular demand for power-
related components, such as power supply units (PSUs), battery backup units
(BBUs), capacitor bank units (CBUs), coolant distribution units (CDUs), and so on.
Moreover, the requirement for efficiency will inevitably necessitate the adoption of
800/±400 voltage direct current (VDC)  in the power rack/center/block

configuration (initially from late 2026 and broadly in 2028, we estimate), eventually
extending to the deployment of  solid state transformers (SST) in the longer term –
all pointing to a more centralized power delivery architecture in the future. This
trend of aggregation could raise the technology barriers amid the fusion of white/
grey   space, favoring players with better capabilities and execution. Overall, we
forecast the datacenter PSU market size to grow at a 75% three-year CAGR to reach

US$33bn in 2028.
Fuel cells to improve flexibility and resilience of AI power delivery: Despite the
strong AI capex outlook (MSe global cloud capex +97%/20% YoY in 2026/2027),
power has become a bottleneck, indicated by MS's forecast power shortfall of 5GW
in 2026, scaling to a total 72GW through 2029 (see report). Fuel cells, among other
alternative energy sources, could emerge as a good solution to pick up some slack
and enhance efficiency through active micro grid management. Those who are able

to offer comprehensive solutions from power sources to power delivery could be
increasingly more competitive in the market.
Assuming coverage of Delta and Lite-On:
• Delta (OW, PT NT$2,500, based on 30x 2027/2028 P/E) should maintain its
dominant position in the industry with its integrated power/thermal related
solutions while fuel cells could add incremental growth opportunities in the
longer term. We think a 30x P/E target multiple is justified by our estimated

63% earnings CAGR in 2025-28, driven by a 44% revenue CAGR and
operating margin expansion to 22.1% in 2028 vs 15.1% in 2025,
• Lite-On (EW, PT NT$315, based on 17x 2027/2028 P/E), the second-largest
player in AI power-related products, should also ride the tailwinds despite its
slower progress  in HVDC power racks and liquid cooling solutions. We think
a 17x P/E  target multiple is justified by our estimated 47% earnings CAGR in
2025-28 (29% revenue CAGR and 6.8ppt OPM expansion  to 16.8% in 2028).

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.

Morgan Stanley Research 3
Executive Summary
AI cluster performance evolving to fulfill  demand
The rapid adoption of artificial intelligence is driving a significant increase in global data
center compute demand. Advances in foundation models, reasoning capabilities, agentic
AI, and enterprise AI deployment are increasing the computational resources required for
both training and inference. To support this growth, hyperscalers and model developers

continue to deploy larger and more powerful AI clusters, driving demand for greater
compute, memory, and networking capacity.
As AI infrastructure scales, cluster performance is increasingly determined by the
efficiency of the entire system rather than the capabilities of individual processors alone.
Compute, networking, cooling, and power infrastructure must all evolve together to
support higher rack densities and larger deployments. While semiconductor innovation

remains critical, supporting infrastructure is becoming an equally important factor in
enabling future AI growth.
AI cluster performance is fundamentally a power problem
As AI clusters continue to scale in size and performance, power consumption is becoming
one of the most important constraints on future infrastructure deployment. The power
challenge is no longer limited to GPUs alone, as modern AI clusters comprise tightly
integrated compute, networking, memory, storage, and cooling systems, all of which

require increasing amounts of power as performance requirements rise. Consequently, the
next phase of AI infrastructure investment should be viewed not only as a semiconductor
upgrade cycle but also as a broad-based power infrastructure upgrade cycle.
Server power consumption has increased significantly across successive AI hardware
generations. Nvidia's architecture roadmap illustrates the industry's migration from
relatively modest server-level power budgets under the Ampere and Hopper generations

toward rack-scale systems under Blackwell and future Rubin architectures. Estimated rack-
level power consumption has increased from roughly 40kW for Hopper systems to
approximately 120kW for GB200 NVL72 and 140kW for GB300 NVL72, with future Vera
Rubin systems expected to approach 200-300kW per rack with current Vera Rubin
systems reaching up to 330kW per rack while future Vera Rubin generations are expected
to surpass these levels. As compute density continues to increase, power delivery

infrastructure is becoming a critical enabler of system performance.
The increase in power consumption is not limited to compute hardware. Networking
systems are also becoming increasingly power intensive as switch bandwidth scales from
100G architectures toward 800G and 1.6T generations. Despite substantial process-node
improvements, switch ASIC power consumption has continued to rise due to increasing
complexity and higher SerDes speeds. As a result, networking equipment now requires

significantly larger power budgets than previous generations, creating additional demand
for higher-capacity power supplies throughout the data center.

Asia Pacific Insight

The growth in power consumption is driving a corresponding upgrade cycle for power-
supply units (PSUs). Earlier AI server generations typically relied on multiple 3-3.3KW
power supplies installed directly within individual server chassis. As the industry
transitions toward rack-scale architectures, power conversion is increasingly centralized
into dedicated power shelves capable of supporting substantially larger power loads. In
Blackwell systems, power shelves utilize 5.5KW power supplies, while future Rubin-

generation platforms are expected to increase PSU ratings to approximately 18KW,
representing a significant increase in both PSU content and power density.
Delivering higher power within a similar footprint creates significant engineering
challenges. Increasing PSU power density requires advances across multiple areas,
including higher-performance power semiconductors such as silicon carbide (SiC) and
gallium nitride (GaN), more efficient power-conversion topologies, smaller and more

capable magnetic components, improved thermal-management solutions, and greater
system integration. As output power increases, thermal constraints become increasingly
important, making heat dissipation, airflow optimization, and mechanical design critical
determinants of PSU performance.
Consequently, on top of the volume driven growth, the power-supply industry is
benefiting from the technology driven dynamics, where efficiency, power density, and

thermal performance are becoming key competitive differentiators. As AI rack power
requirements continue to increase, PSU innovation will become an increasingly important
component of the broader AI infrastructure investment cycle.

Complementary hardware for more resilient power delivery
As AI clusters become more power intensive, maintaining stable and resilient power
delivery is emerging as a critical design priority. Traditional centralized power

architectures are increasingly being complemented by localized energy-storage and
power-conditioning technologies that can respond rapidly to dynamic workload demands.
Battery Backup Units (BBUs) represent one of the most important additions to modern AI
power architectures. Positioned close to the computing workload, BBUs provide localized
energy storage capable of responding significantly faster than facility-level backup
systems. Their role extends beyond conventional backup power functionality. BBUs can

### Page 6

M
Asia Pacific Insight
6
absorb transient fluctuations, smooth power ramps, and improve overall rack-level power
stability. By providing local buffering capacity, BBUs help reduce the burden on upstream
power distribution infrastructure while improving overall system resilience.
Exhibit 11: Concept of peak shaving for AI server power loads
Source: Nvidia
Exhibit 12: BBU
Source: Panasonic
Capacitor Backup Units (CBUs) address an even shorter-duration segment of the power

stability challenge. Utilizing supercapacitors and other high-power-density storage
technologies, CBUs can deliver and absorb power within milliseconds, making them
particularly effective for managing rapid GPU power spikes. These systems act as shock
absorbers within the electrical architecture, preventing sudden load changes from
propagating throughout the broader power network. As AI rack power densities continue
increasing, capacitor-based energy storage is becoming an attractive solution for

improving power quality without requiring substantial oversizing of facility infrastructure.
Exhibit 13: CBU
Source: Panasonic
Exhibit 14: GB300 power shelf with integrated capacitors
Source: Nvidia
Together, BBUs and CBUs provide complementary benefits. BBUs are designed to handle
longer-duration events, while CBUs are optimized for extremely fast transient response.
The integration of these technologies enables operators to improve power quality, reduce

stranded infrastructure capacity, enhance system reliability, and increase effective
utilization of expensive electrical assets. As rack densities continue to rise, localized
energy-storage solutions are likely to become a standard component of AI data center
design rather than an optional enhancement.

### Page 7

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 7
The adoption of BBU and CBU architectures may also enable future increases in rack
density. As operators attempt to push toward several hundred kilowatts per rack,
localized power-buffering solutions can help mitigate transient peaks and reduce stress on
grid-facing infrastructure. This allows operators to deploy higher-density computing
resources without proportionately increasing upstream electrical capacity.

HVDC emerging as a necessity, with SST in the pipeline
800VDC and ±400VDC progressing simultaneously
The rapid increase in AI rack power density is creating growing challenges for conventional
power-distribution architectures. As rack-level power consumption rises from tens of
kilowatts to several hundreds of KWs, electrical losses, cable size, thermal management
requirements, and overall system complexity increase significantly. Under traditional low-

voltage power architectures, delivering larger amounts of power requires proportionally
higher current, which increases resistive losses and places greater stress on cables,
connectors, busbars, and power-conversion equipment.
At the same time, every power-conversion stage introduces efficiency losses and
generates additional heat. As AI deployments scale into multi-megawatt and eventually
gigawatt-level campuses, even small efficiency improvements can translate into

meaningful reductions in operating costs, cooling requirements, and infrastructure
investment. Consequently, the industry is increasingly focused on power architectures
that can reduce conversion stages, lower current levels, and improve overall power-
delivery efficiency.
Against this backdrop, high-voltage direct current (HVDC) is emerging as one of the most
important architectural upgrades for next-generation AI infrastructure. By increasing
distribution voltage, HVDC allows the same amount of power to be delivered at a lower

current, reducing conduction losses and improving system efficiency. Higher-voltage
architectures also improve scalability for ultra-high-density rack deployments by
alleviating some of the physical and thermal constraints associated with conventional
power-distribution systems.

### Page 8

M
Asia Pacific Insight
8
Exhibit 16: Better power efficiency for 800VDC architecture
Source: Delta
Exhibit 17: Better efficiency leads to cost savings
Source: Delta
Nvidia's roadmap illustrates the industry's gradual migration toward higher-voltage power
architectures. Earlier AI server generations primarily utilized conventional power-delivery
schemes centered around rack-level 48V distribution. As rack power requirements
increased with Blackwell-generation systems, industry participants began evaluating

higher-voltage architectures capable of supporting substantially greater power density.
Looking ahead, the industry is increasingly converging around 800VDC as a potential
next-generation standard for large-scale AI deployments, particularly as rack-level power
consumption approaches several hundred kilowatts. The transition reflects the need to
improve power-distribution efficiency while supporting the continued growth of AI
computing infrastructure.

We believe that the adoption of the 800VDC power rack could start from late 4Q26,
though it should be small scope deployment through 2027, since the in-rack power shelf
is the reference design in the Vera Rubin NVL72 rack. Broader adoption should happen
more likely from late 2027 and 2028, as the Vera Rubin Ultra rack further pushes up the
power consumption.
Exhibit 18: HVDC deployment roadmap
Source: Nvidia

### Page 9

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 9
The 800VDC power rack BOM is primarily driven by the PSU, which accounts for 50–55%
of total cost, followed by the BBU at 25–30%, with other components comprising the
remaining 15–20%. This cost structure reflects the broader functionality integrated into an
HVDC power rack, combining power conversion, backup power and supporting
components within a centralized architecture.
For power supply players, one of the most direct implications is that an HVDC power rack

carries around 4–5x higher dollar content per watt compared with a conventional in-rack
PSU solution, since the former integrates more components into a robust system and
power supply players usually ship the entire rack instead of just the components.

Source: Morgan Stanley Research
However, the industry has not yet fully converged on a single architecture. While Nvidia's
roadmap increasingly points toward 800VDC, several hyperscalers and members of the
Open Compute Project (OCP) ecosystem have expressed interest in ±400VDC
architectures. Both approaches deliver similar total voltage levels and pursue many of the

same objectives, including lower current, higher efficiency, and improved scalability. The
debate largely centers on implementation trade-offs rather than end goals.
Supporters of 800VDC generally highlight the advantages of a simpler single-bus
architecture, potentially lower system complexity, and closer alignment with existing
industrial high-voltage DC equipment. The architecture avoids the need to maintain
balanced positive and negative voltage rails and may simplify certain aspects of power

conversion and system integration. These characteristics make 800VDC particularly
attractive in environments where operational simplicity and interoperability with existing
electrical infrastructure are key priorities.
Advocates of ±400VDC, meanwhile, emphasize the advantages of a bipolar architecture.
By creating positive and negative voltage rails around a neutral reference point, ±400VDC
can potentially reduce insulation requirements, simplify certain fault-management

scenarios, and provide improved flexibility for load balancing and future system expansion.
Many hyperscalers also view the bipolar approach as being more compatible with long-
term visions for highly distributed power systems incorporating energy storage, renewable
energy, and microgrid functionality.
Importantly, the ultimate beneficiaries may be largely independent of which standard
gains wider adoption. With the exception of certain power-semiconductor and protection-

system requirements, many component suppliers across the power ecosystem remain

### Page 10

M
Asia Pacific Insight
10
broadly agnostic between 800VDC and ±400VDC implementations. Higher-voltage
architectures in either form require similar upgrades in power conversion, power
management, cooling, connectors, magnetics, and infrastructure equipment. As a result,
the industry's migration toward HVDC matters significantly more than the eventual
selection of any single voltage standard.

Solid-state transformers to follow after 800VDC/±400VDC matures
Looking further ahead, solid-state transformers (SSTs) represent a potential next-
generation evolution of power infrastructure. Unlike traditional transformers that operate

primarily as passive devices, SSTs leverage power electronics to provide voltage
conversion, power conditioning, and bidirectional energy management within a single
platform. This enables greater controllability and flexibility while supporting increasingly
digitalized power networks.
Although SSTs remain at an early stage of commercialization and face challenges related
to economics, reliability, and large-scale deployment, the technology aligns well with long-

term data center trends. As AI campuses evolve into utility-scale power consumers
incorporating energy storage, distributed generation, and advanced power management,
SSTs could eventually become a key enabling technology.
Therefore, while HVDC is expected to drive the next phase of power architecture
upgrades, SSTs represent a longer-term technology roadmap that could further transform
how future data centers distribute and manage electricity.

Fuel cells for better flexibility and resilience
Fuel cells generate electricity through an electrochemical reaction rather than
combustion. Hydrogen, natural gas, biogas, or other fuels are supplied to the fuel cell
stack, where chemical energy is converted directly into electricity, heat, and in some cases
water. Unlike batteries, which store energy, fuel cells continuously generate power as long
as fuel is supplied.
Exhibit 25: Fuel cell diagram

Source: 3M
Fuel cells can offer several benefits, including:
• High energy efficiency: Direct electrochemical conversion enables higher
efficiency than conventional combustion-based power generation, reducing fuel
consumption and operating costs.
• Low carbon emissions: Fuel cells produce minimal direct emissions and can
support long-term decarbonization goals when powered by low-carbon fuels such
as hydrogen.
• Grid independence and energy security: Provides reliable onsite power

generation, reducing dependence on grid availability and improving energy
resilience.
• Long-duration energy storage enabler: Hydrogen-based fuel-cell systems can
complement renewable energy by storing energy over extended periods,
supporting long-duration and seasonal energy storage.
• Fuel flexibility: Certain fuel-cell technologies can operate on hydrogen, natural
gas, biogas, ammonia-derived hydrogen, and other hydrogen-rich fuels, allowing
deployment using existing fuel infrastructure.

• Low maintenance and quiet operation: Limited moving parts reduce maintenance
requirements while providing quieter operation than conventional combustion-
based generators.
• Scalable deployment: Can be deployed across a wide range of power levels, from
distributed backup systems to utility-scale installations, making fuel cells suitable
for large AI data center campuses

### Page 13

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 13
However, the following challenging are hurdles for wider deployment:
• High system cost: Fuel cells remain more expensive than conventional power-
generation technologies, limiting large-scale adoption.
• Limited hydrogen infrastructure: Hydrogen production, transportation, storage,
and refueling networks remain underdeveloped in most regions.
• Durability requirements: Long-term reliability and stack lifetime remain key

challenges, particularly for large-scale and mission-critical deployments.
• Regulatory and safety considerations: Hydrogen handling requires specialized
infrastructure and compliance with stringent safety regulations, increasing
deployment complexity.
• Lack of standardization: Multiple fuel-cell technologies compete across different
applications, creating uncertainty around long-term technology standards and
adoption paths.
Fuel cells have the potential to fundamentally reshape future data center power

architectures. Traditionally, data centers rely on grid electricity as the primary power
source and diesel generators for backup power. However, as AI-driven workloads
significantly increase power demand and power availability becomes a key deployment
constraint, hyperscalers are increasingly evaluating fuel cells not only as a backup-power
solution but also as a potential primary power source. This could accelerate the transition
from a grid-centric architecture toward one incorporating distributed onsite generation,

enabling data center deployment in regions where utility power capacity is limited.
Over the longer term, fuel cells could become a core component of AI data center
microgrids that integrate renewable generation, battery storage, hydrogen storage, and
intelligent energy-management systems. Such an architecture would improve scalability,
resilience, and energy independence while reducing reliance on centralized utility
infrastructure. In addition, because fuel cells inherently generate DC power, they align

well with the industry's migration toward HVDC-based power distribution architectures.
This could simplify power delivery by reducing conversion stages between generation and
IT equipment, improving overall system efficiency and supporting next-generation high-
density AI deployment
Exhibit 26: Fuel cell in datacenter power delivery architecture
Source: Delta

Morgan Stanley Research 17
AI Cluster Performance Evolving to Fulfill Demand
AI adoption driving exponential growth in compute demand
AI is becoming a mainstream computing workload
Over the past three years, generative AI has evolved from a research application into a
core technology platform. Consumer applications such as ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and
Copilot now serve hundreds of millions of users globally, while enterprises are

increasingly embedding AI into software development, customer service, document
analysis, enterprise search, healthcare, financial research, and industrial automation.
Historically, software demand scaled roughly with the number of users. AI demand is
fundamentally different because every user interaction consumes computational
resources in real time. Unlike traditional cloud applications, where the bulk of
computation occurs during software development, AI applications continuously consume

compute whenever a model generates a response. Consequently, rising AI adoption
directly translates into higher demand for data center compute resources.
Model complexity continues to increase
The AI industry has historically improved model performance through scaling laws: larger
models trained on larger datasets using more compute generally produce better
outcomes. While efficiency improvements continue, leading AI developers remain
committed to scaling model capabilities.

More importantly, the industry is moving beyond conventional language models toward
significantly more compute-intensive architectures.
Reasoning models
Traditional LLMs generate answers directly from a prompt. New reasoning models
perform multiple internal computational steps before producing a response. Instead of
generating a few hundred output tokens, advanced reasoning systems may generate
thousands or even tens of thousands of internal reasoning tokens while evaluating

different solution paths. This dramatically increases inference workloads.
The industry is increasingly shifting from "parameter scaling" toward "test-time compute
scaling," where additional compute is consumed during inference to improve answer
quality. From an infrastructure perspective, this is highly significant because inference
workloads occur continuously rather than once during model training.

### Page 18

M
Asia Pacific Insight
18
Exhibit 28: Increasing demand for compute power from reasoning models
1x
2-5x
5-20x
10-50x
Basic chatbot response Coding assistant Reasonaing model Multiagent work flow
Relative Compute Requirement
Source: Morgan Stanley Research estimates
Agentic AI
The next wave of AI applications involves autonomous agents capable of planning,
executing, verifying, and refining tasks.
Instead of generating a single answer, an AI agent may search multiple information

sources, run code, validate results, generate follow-up tasks, and produce a final response.
Each step requires additional inference cycles.
A workflow that previously required one model invocation may now require dozens of
model calls, substantially increasing compute consumption per user.
Multimodal AI
AI models are increasingly processing images, video, audio, sensor data, and text
simultaneously.
Video understanding is particularly compute intensive because each second of video

contains vastly more information than a text prompt.
As multimodal AI adoption grows, compute demand per query rises significantly, creating
another structural driver of accelerator demand.
Training demand remains robust
Although inference is becoming the largest source of AI compute demand, training
workloads continue to expand. Frontier model development increasingly requires clusters
containing tens of thousands of accelerators operating simultaneously.

Training runs are becoming larger because developers are pursuing larger parameter
counts, more training data, longer training durations, multimodal datasets and
reinforcement learning and reasoning optimization
Consequently, each new generation of foundation models typically consumes
substantially more compute than the previous generation.

### Page 19

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 19
Importantly, training infrastructure sets the baseline for future inference deployment.
Every major model developer must maintain access to large-scale training clusters to
remain competitive, supporting continued demand for high-end AI accelerators.
More compute requests to be fulfilled by better chip
performance
Rising compute demand cannot be addressed simply by adding more servers. Modern AI
workloads increasingly encounter bottlenecks related to memory bandwidth, interconnect

performance, power consumption, and cooling. As a result, each new generation of AI
hardware delivers improvements across multiple dimensions.
Compute performance
The latest AI accelerators provide several-fold performance improvements versus prior
generations. For example, NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture was specifically designed to
support both large-scale training and high-volume inference, with significant gains in AI
throughput compared with previous Hopper-based systems. Performance improvements

are necessary because reasoning and agentic workloads require substantially more
operations per query.
Memory capacity and bandwidth
AI models are increasingly constrained by memory rather than raw compute, because
longer models, longer context windows, reasoning models and agentic workflows all
require more memory. As model complexity increases, modern high-end accelerators
usually incorporate hundreds of gigabytes of High Bandwidth Memory (HBM), providing

memory bandwidth measured in multiple terabytes per second.
Interconnect performance
As AI clusters scale from hundreds to thousands of accelerators, communication between
processors becomes increasingly important. A large AI training cluster effectively behaves
like a single massive computer. Consequently, networking technologies such as NVLink,
InfiniBand, Ethernet, etc., have become critical enablers of AI scaling.
Many AI workloads can no longer be accelerated simply by increasing GPU performance;

cluster interconnect bandwidth must also improve.

AI Cluster Performance Is Fundamentally a Power Problem
As AI clusters become larger and more powerful, every component within the cluster
must deliver higher throughput, lower latency, and greater bandwidth. Achieving these
improvements inevitably requires more electrical power.
Therefore, the power challenge is no longer limited to GPUs. Modern AI clusters are highly
integrated systems comprising compute servers, networking fabrics, memory subsystems,

storage infrastructure, and cooling equipment. As cluster performance increases, power
consumption rises across the entire infrastructure stack.
In our view, the next phase of AI infrastructure investment should be viewed not simply as
a semiconductor upgrade cycle, but as a broad-based power consumption cycle driven by
the need for ever-higher AI cluster performance.
Server systems seeing increasing TDP
Nvidia’s AI-server power roadmap is best understood as a transition from chip-defined, air-

cooled nodes to power-, cooling- and fabric-constrained rack-scale computers: the
Ampere-era A100 SXM carried a disclosed 400W standard TDP—up to 500W in custom
thermal designs—with 40/80GB HBM2/HBM2e and 600GB/s NVLink, while the eight-
GPU, dual-AMD-EPYC DGX A100 was a conventional 6U server with a 6.5KW maximum
input rating, meaning GPU silicon represented only roughly half of the node envelope
once CPUs, NVSwitches, memory, NICs, drives, fans and conversion losses were included.

Hopper stepped the H100 SXM to a disclosed 700W TDP, 80GB HBM3, roughly 3TB/s
memory bandwidth and 900GB/s fourth-generation NVLink, while H200 retained
essentially the same Hopper compute architecture and power profile but substituted
141GB of faster HBM3e at 4.8TB/s—making H200 primarily a memory-capacity/bandwidth
upgrade rather than a compute-TDP step-up—and Nvidia rates the eight-GPU, dual-Xeon
DGX H100/H200 at 10.2KW maximum input, versus 6.5KW for DGX A100;

The architectural discontinuity arrives with GB200. Rather than treating an eight-GPU
server as the scale-up unit and using InfiniBand/Ethernet primarily between nodes, Grace
Blackwell NVL72 makes the entire rack the scale-up domain, integrating 18 one-U compute
trays—each with two 72-core Grace Arm CPUs and four Blackwell GPUs—plus nine
NVLink switch trays, management switches, , a 50–51V DC busbar, copper NVLink
cartridges and liquid manifolds, so that 36 CPUs and 72 GPUs operate as one low-latency

NVLink-connected system. Based on the same compute die, Nvidia upgraded the HBM
content to further improve the performance of GB300 chip. Overall, the rack level power
consumption is estimated at ~120KW for the GB200 NVL72 and ~140KW for the GB300
NVL72.
Vera Rubin—often called VR200/R200 in industry roadmaps—pushes the same rack-
level philosophy further. Nvidia’s disclosed architecture combines 72 dual-die Rubin GPUs
with 36 new Vera CPUs, replacing Grace’s 72 Arm Neoverse cores with 88 custom

Olympus cores, raising CPU memory capacity and bandwidth to as much as 1.5TB
LPDDR5X and 1.2TB/s, and doubling coherent CPU–GPU NVLink-C2C bandwidth to 1.8TB/
s; Rubin itself moves from HBM3e to 288GB HBM4 at up to 22TB/s, introduces a third-

### Page 22

M
Asia Pacific Insight
22
generation Transformer Engine and raises sixth-generation NVLink scale-up bandwidth to
3.6TB/s per GPU, while ConnectX-9 provides 1.6Tbps/GPU scale-out connectivity and
BlueField-4 increasingly offloads storage, security and infrastructure processing.
Nvidia has not published a simple Rubin chip TDP in its primary datasheet, but our supply
chain checks indicate roughly 1.8kW at an efficiency-oriented operating point and up to

approximately 2.3kW at the performance mode, which is consistently corroborated by
system vendors, with ASUS specifying 187kW Max-Q and 227kW Max-P for its fully liquid-
cooled Vera Rubin NVL72.
Looking ahead, we expect this trend to extend into the future along with the technology
advancement and more critical workloads.

Networking consuming more power as well
The network switch system is another key devices within the datacenter and has been
evolving  over the past years, driven primarily by advances in the switch ASIC. From
Tomahawk 1 (2014) to Tomahawk 6 (2025), the process node shrank from 28nm to 3nm,
while aggregate switching bandwidth increased from 3.2 Tbps to 102.4 Tbps, representing
a 32× increase. Over the same period, SerDes speed improved from 25 Gbps NRZ to 200

Gbps PAM4, enabling maximum port speeds to scale from 100G to 1.6T.
While process scaling significantly improved switching efficiency, it did not prevent ASIC
power from rising. ASIC TDP increased from 150 W to 800 W, reflecting the growing
complexity of higher radix switches and faster SerDes interfaces.
The impact of ASIC scaling is evident at the system level. As switch bandwidth increased
from 100G-class systems to 800G and 1.6T platforms, power delivery requirements grew

dramatically. Representative PSU ratings increased from 850W in Tomahawk 2 systems to
3,200W in Tomahawk 6 systems, nearly a 4× increase.
The rise in PSU power is driven not only by the higher ASIC TDP, but also by the growing
power consumption of high-speed optics, increased cooling requirements, and larger
overall system power budgets. As systems evolved from 100G to 1.6T ports, switch
vendors were forced to add more fan redundancy, larger heatsinks, and in some cases

larger chassis footprints. For example, Tomahawk 6 systems have expanded to 4U form
factors with 7+1 fan redundancy to accommodate the thermal load associated with 102.4
Tbps switching capacity.
Alongside the increasing power consumption of the server racks, network switches are
also witnessing higher power budgets  as well.

Power supply growing on both volume and specs
PSU spec upgrade trend
In response to the increasing power consumption of the AI servers, the power supply
architecture has been migrating as well. in the A100 and H100/200 single system servers,
6 power supply units are placed directly inside the chassis, with each capable to deliver
3.0-3.3KW of power.
When Nvidia make a broad based move to the rack level server solution from the

Blackwell generation, the power supply units were no longer placed directly within the
chassis housing the HPCs that consume power; instead, power supply units are separated
and grouped into power shelves. In the GB200/300 systems, there are 8 power shelves
sitting on the same rack as the compute trays and switch trays, each enclosing six 5.5KW
power supply units.
For the upcoming Vera Rubin rack, the power supply unit will be upgraded to 18.3KW of

output to comprise a power shelf of 110KW, and each power shelf will take 3U of space on
the rack vs. 1U in the Blackwell generation.

Power density per PSU (KW)

Challenges increasing the power density of PSUs
To come up with power supply unit with higher power density, manufacturers might need

to solve the following potential hurdles:
Better power semiconductors (SiC and GaN)
The biggest improvement in PSU power density comes from replacing traditional silicon
power chips with newer materials such as silicon carbide (SiC) and gallium nitride (GaN).
These devices waste less energy during power conversion and can operate at much higher
speeds. As a result, many supporting components inside the PSU—such as transformers
and inductors—can be made significantly smaller. This allows PSU manufacturers to fit

more output power into the same physical size while also improving efficiency. In many
ways, SiC and GaN have become the foundation for the latest generation of high-density
AI server power supplies.

### Page 25

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 25
More efficient power conversion designs
Modern PSUs use more sophisticated circuit designs, i.e.,  bridgeless PFC vs. conventional
one, that reduce energy losses during power conversion. A conventional boost PFC
includes a diode bridge and therefore incurs multiple semiconductor conduction drops. A
bridgeless totem-pole PFC reduces the number of devices in the active current path and is
now a preferred architecture for dense server PSUs. Instead of simply converting

electricity from one voltage to another, newer architectures are designed to minimize
wasted energy throughout the process. Higher efficiency means less heat is generated,
which directly enables higher power density. This is particularly important in AI servers,
where even small efficiency improvements can translate into meaningful reductions in
heat generation and cooling requirements.
Smaller and smarter magnetic components
Transformers and inductors are often among the largest components inside a PSU. A

major area of innovation is reducing their size without sacrificing performance.
Manufacturers are increasingly using planar magnetics, where copper traces on circuit
boards replace traditional wire windings. These designs are thinner, easier to cool, and
more space-efficient. Some suppliers are also combining multiple magnetic functions into
a single component, further reducing overall volume. As PSU power levels increase from 3
KW to 5.5 KW and beyond, shrinking magnetic components becomes one of the most

important contributors to power-density improvement.
Improved thermal management
As power output increases, heat becomes the primary obstacle to higher density. Even
highly efficient PSUs still generate a significant amount of heat. Therefore, manufacturers
are investing heavily in better cooling solutions, including optimized airflow paths,
advanced heatsinks, vapor chambers, and liquid-cooling technologies. The goal is to
remove heat more effectively so that components can operate safely even when packed

more tightly together. In many modern PSU designs, thermal engineering is just as
important as electrical engineering in determining achievable power density.
Higher component integration
Another way to increase power density is to reduce the amount of unused space inside
the PSU. Manufacturers are integrating more functions into fewer components and using
three-dimensional layouts that make better use of available volume. Components are
stacked, arranged vertically, or combined into integrated modules to maximize space

utilization. This approach is similar to how smartphones have become more powerful
without becoming larger—the same principle is increasingly being applied to server power
supplies.
Higher efficiency creates more thermal headroom
Efficiency improvements provide a double benefit. First, less electricity is wasted. Second,
less heat is generated. For example, a 5.5 kW PSU operating at 97.5% efficiency generates
roughly 30% less heat than one operating at 96.5% efficiency. While a one-percentage-

point improvement may appear small, it can remove tens of watts of heat from the
system. This allows engineers to either increase output power within the same form factor
or reduce cooling requirements while maintaining existing power levels.

### Page 26

M
Asia Pacific Insight
26
Reducing the size of energy storage components
Server PSUs contain capacitors that store energy and maintain power delivery during
short interruptions. Historically, these capacitors occupied a significant amount of space.
Newer designs use smarter control techniques that allow manufacturers to achieve the
same performance with smaller capacitor banks. Reducing capacitor volume frees up
valuable internal space that can be used for higher power output or additional

functionality.
For example, in Infineon’s 3.3 KW example, the calculated capacitance for a conventional
10ms hold-up implementation was approximately 2.5 mF; allowing deeper discharge
through a baby-boost stage reduced the requirement to about 900 µF. The 12 KW design
similarly uses a bidirectional buck-boost buffer to achieve 20ms hold-up at full load
without the capacitor volume that a passive solution would require. The penalty is
additional semiconductors, an inductor, control software and failure modes, but the net

volumetric benefit can be substantial.
Advanced mechanical design
Power density is ultimately measured in watts per unit volume, making physical packaging
extremely important. Manufacturers continuously optimize component placement, airflow
channels, connector design, and enclosure structures to eliminate unused space. The best
PSU designs are often not defined by a single breakthrough component but rather by
hundreds of small mechanical optimizations that collectively improve volumetric

efficiency.
Datacenter PSU market size to grow at a 75% CAGR in 2025-28
Overall, we estimate the global datacenter power supply market will grow at a three-year
CAGR of 75% to reach US$33bn in 2028 from US$6bn in 2025, driven mainly by server
shipment growth, increasing power consumption per rack, and the adoption of HVDC
power racks from 2027.

Morgan Stanley Research 27
Complementary Hardware for More Resilient Power
Delivery
Transient power spikes at AI servers
AI servers have a much more dynamic power profile than conventional enterprise servers
because GPU utilization changes sharply throughout a workload. During large-model
training, thousands of GPUs typically operate in a bulk-synchronous manner, and power
consumption approaches the accelerator’s design limit during tensor-compute phases,

then falls during collective communications, check pointing, data movement, or periods in
which GPUs wait for slower devices to synchronize. Inference can create a similar pattern
as request arrivals, batching, context length and token-generation activity change rapidly.
Because the GPUs perform these transitions together, the power variation of one
accelerator is multiplied across the server, rack and cluster rather than being averaged out
by unrelated workloads, so the magnitude of the challenge is also rising with system

density.  Power spikes are therefore not simply the result of higher average consumption;
they reflect the combination of higher accelerator power, more GPUs per system and
increasingly synchronized workload behavior.
Exhibit 35: Transient power spikes at AI servers
Source: Infineon
System performance and reliability potentially compromised
The key issue is that power delivery systems are designed to supply a relatively stable
amount of power, while AI workloads can change power demand almost instantaneously.

When the load changes faster than the power-delivery system can react, a temporary
mismatch occurs between power demanded by the GPUs and power supplied by the PSU,
creating electrical instability throughout the server.

### Page 28

M
Asia Pacific Insight
28
Sudden increases in GPU demand lead to voltage drops
When GPUs transition from a low-utilization state (communication, synchronization,
checkpointing, etc.) to a full-compute state, their current demand can increase within
microseconds. However, the PSU cannot instantly increase its output because its control
loop requires time to detect the load change and adjust power conversion.
During this response period, the additional energy must come from capacitors located

throughout the power chain. As these capacitors discharge, the voltage on the DC bus
begins to fall, creating voltage drop.
If the drop becomes sufficiently large, several operational issues can occur:
• GPU throttling: The GPU reduces clock speed or performance to remain within safe
operating limits.
• Computation errors: Voltage instability can affect timing margins in high-speed
processors and memory.
• Unexpected resets or shutdowns: Protection circuits may trigger if voltage falls

below specified thresholds.
• Training interruption: Large AI training jobs running across thousands of GPUs may
fail if even a small number of nodes experience instability.
In a large AI cluster, a single server failure can force the entire training job to restart from
the latest checkpoint, resulting in significant productivity loss.
Sudden decreases in GPU demand results in voltage spike
The opposite problem occurs when GPUs rapidly reduce power consumption.

For example, after a compute-intensive phase, GPUs may enter a communication phase
where power consumption drops sharply. The PSU, however, is still delivering power
based on the previous load condition. The excess energy has nowhere to go immediately
and accumulates in the DC bus, causing a temporary voltage spike.
This can create:
• Overvoltage stress on components
• Protection-circuit activation
• Accelerated degradation of capacitors and voltage regulators

• Higher risk of component failure over time
Although individual spikes may be small, AI servers can experience these transitions
continuously during training, causing repeated stress throughout the power-delivery chain.
Excessive current surges throughout the system
Power spikes are not only a voltage problem—they also create large current excursions.
A 1KW increase in power demand at 12V requires roughly 83A of additional current. In
modern AI servers, where multiple GPUs can change utilization simultaneously, current

swings can reach hundreds of amperes within a very short period.

### Page 29

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 29
These current surges lead to:
• Higher conductor losses
• Increased connector heating
• Greater stress on voltage-regulator modules (VRMs)
• Larger thermal fluctuations in power components
• As GPU power ratings rise to over 1KW, the magnitude of these transient currents
becomes increasingly difficult to manage.
Thermal cycling and reliability degradation
Perhaps the most overlooked issue is the impact on long-term reliability.

Every power excursion creates a corresponding temperature excursion. When power rises,
semiconductor junction temperatures increase; when power falls, temperatures decrease.
This repeated heating and cooling cycle occurs thousands or even millions of times over a
system's lifetime.
The result is accelerated wear on:
• Electrolytic capacitors
• Power MOSFETs
• Solder joints
• PCB interconnects
• Connectors and busbars
Consequently, even if average power remains within specification, transient power spikes

can shorten component lifetime and increase failure rates.
Infrastructure overdesign and lower efficiency
To prevent instability, operators often provision the power infrastructure for the worst-
case peak rather than average consumption.
For example:
• A rack averaging 80KW may need infrastructure sized for 120-140KW transient
peaks.
• PSUs must be oversized to accommodate short-duration excursions.
• UPS systems must reserve capacity for sudden load increases.

• Cooling systems must handle peak thermal output.
This creates a significant amount of stranded capacity, where expensive electrical and
cooling infrastructure remains underutilized most of the time. Furthermore, PSUs and
UPS systems typically operate most efficiently near their optimal loading point; oversized
systems therefore tend to operate at lower efficiency, increasing operating costs.

### Page 30

M
Asia Pacific Insight
30
Conventional power delivery systems not designed to deal
with power volatility
In the conventional power delivery architecture, the UPS protects the data hall against
utility outages and power-quality disturbances, while the PSU converts upstream power
into the tightly regulated DC output required by the server. Both remain essential in the
modern AI server clusters, but neither was originally optimized to absorb large, repetitive

and synchronized AI load steps economically.
The PSU can use its internal capacitors to cover the initial transient, but capacitor volume
is limited; once that stored energy is depleted, the PSU control loop must raise input
current, potentially producing current overshoot and transferring the disturbance
upstream.
The UPS is located farther from the accelerator and sees the aggregated load of many
PSUs. A transient originating near the GPU can therefore occur on microsecond-to-

millisecond timescales—including permitted GPU electrical-design-power excursions
around 50 ms—before the complete upstream conversion chain can rebalance power.
This is a response time and location mismatch, rather than evidence that UPSs or PSUs are
fundamentally incapable of supporting AI. New AI-tolerant UPS platforms are being
designed for faster and larger load steps, but many legacy systems may still experience
voltage excursions, nuisance alarms, lower partial-load efficiency or unwanted bypass

transfers under steep synchronized loads.
To complement the conventional power delivery architecture, more AI server clusters are
adopting BBUs or CBUs to shave peak loads and  mitigate the above-mentioned issues.
Exhibit 36: Concept of peak shaving for AI server power loads
Source: Nvidia
Battery backup units  seeing increasing importance
What's a BBU?
A BBU is an integrated battery, power-conversion and control system that supplies DC
power to server loads when the normal power source is interrupted or temporarily

insufficient. In an OCP-style rack, the BBU modules are housed in a dedicated shelf located
near the PSU power shelf and connect directly to the common DC busbar. Under normal
conditions, the PSUs power the servers and maintain the BBU’s charge; when the bus

### Page 31

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 31
voltage falls below a programmed threshold, the BBU’s bidirectional converter changes
from charging or standby into discharge mode and supports the rack. Analog Devices’
ORV3 reference design detects a bus voltage below 48.5V for more than 2ms and is
required to ramp to full support within another 2ms, while maintaining the bus above the
specified minimum voltage.
Key components within a BBU
• Battery-cell pack: The cells store the energy used during discharge. Lithium-ion

cylindrical cells are commonly arranged in series and parallel to obtain the
required voltage, power and runtime; the ORV3 reference module uses an
interchangeable 11-series, 6-parallel battery pack. The cell must provide not only
sufficient energy but also high discharge power, low internal resistance and
predictable performance over its qualified life.
• Bidirectional DC/DC converter: The converter controls energy flow between the
battery pack and the rack DC bus. It steps voltage in the appropriate direction

during charging and discharging and regulates the output so that the battery’s
changing voltage does not destabilize the server bus. Multiphase designs divide the
large current across several parallel power stages; TI’s 12 KW design uses six 2 KW
phases and reports peak efficiency above 99%, including above 99.5% when
battery and bus voltages are close.
• Battery management system: The BMS measures individual cell voltages, current
and temperature, balances cells and estimates state of charge and state of health.

It also protects against overcharge, excessive discharge, overheating and short
circuit. Accurate state-of-health information is important because the operator
must know whether an apparently charged BBU can still deliver its required rack
power and runtime after years of standby operation and periodic discharge events.
• Power-path and protection circuitry: Fuses, current sensors, disconnect switches,
protection MOSFETs and hot-swap circuits isolate faults and prevent uncontrolled

current flow. Typical protections include overvoltage, undervoltage, overcurrent
and overtemperature shutdown. Hot-swap capability allows a failed or end-of-life
module to be replaced without intentionally shutting down the entire rack, while
active current sharing ensures that parallel BBU modules carry an appropriate
portion of the load.
• Control, monitoring and communications: A module controller manages
operating modes, converter control, fault handling, diagnostics and communication

with the BMS and rack controller. Interfaces such as PMBus, Modbus, CAN, RS-485
or Ethernet can expose battery voltage, current, temperature, state of charge,
state of health, fan speed and fault status to data-center management systems.
Analog Devices’ reference platform uses separate controllers for battery
monitoring and overall BBU logic, communications and housekeeping.
• Thermal-management system: Fans, temperature sensors, airflow paths and

mechanical spacing around cells remove heat during high-rate charging and
discharging. Thermal management is both a performance and a safety requirement:
excessive temperature accelerates battery ageing and can restrict available
discharge power. Analog Devices’ ORV3 design therefore incorporates fan control,
airflow simulation and cell-to-cell spacing, while Murata’s commercial 3 KW BBU
includes internal forced-air cooling and overtemperature protection.

### Page 32

M
Asia Pacific Insight
32
• Shelf, backplane and mechanical integration: The shelf distributes DC power and
communications across several modules and provides retention, service access and
cooling. Modularity allows power and runtime to be scaled by adding units and
enables redundancy such as the ORV3 5+1 configuration. Murata specifies a 2U,
hot-swappable 3 KW module, while Eaton’s 19-inch BBU shelf accepts six 2.75 kW
batteries and scales runtime according to the application requirement

Exhibit 37: BBU
Source: Panasonic
Exhibit 38: BBU diagram
Source: ADI
Key functions of BBUs
Backup power and outage ride-through
The BBU’s fundamental function is to maintain rack operation during a brief loss of
upstream power. The ride-through interval gives the data center time to restore the
primary source, transfer to another source, preserve critical data, migrate workloads or
perform an orderly shutdown. Rather than attempting long-duration operation, rack BBUs

are generally optimized for high power over a relatively short period; the ORV3 example
specifies 3KW per module for four minutes, and Murata guarantees a 240-second hold-up
period at full-rated load under stated age and temperature conditions.
For AI systems, this protection is particularly valuable because a rack interruption can
affect a synchronized workload extending well beyond the failed server. Local ride-
through prevents a momentary upstream disturbance from immediately removing GPU,

CPU, networking and storage power from the rack. Infineon consequently describes the
server BBU as an integrated backup supply intended to prevent data loss and maintain AI
workloads during outages or grid failures.
Peak shaving and load smoothing
A BBU can be operated as an active energy buffer. as it charges during lower-demand
periods and discharges when server consumption temporarily exceeds a defined rack or
PSU limit. This allows a relatively stable upstream power source to support a more

volatile downstream AI workload. The economic benefit is that PSU, UPS and electrical
capacity can be designed closer to sustained demand rather than every short theoretical
peak, although the achievable benefit depends on the frequency, duration and
predictability of those peaks.

### Page 33

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 33
The important limitation is battery cycling. A BBU designed primarily for emergency
standby may not be optimized for continuous, repetitive peak shaving, so frequent cycling
affects heat generation, state of charge, usable life and replacement schedules. Very short
and highly repetitive transients may therefore be better supported by a CBU or hybrid
battery-capacitor system, leaving the battery to cover longer events. Renesas

correspondingly distinguishes distributed BBUs from hybrid systems that combine
batteries with capacitors for fast transient response and longer-duration backup.
Power stabilization, redundancy and fault isolation
A rack BBU adds another independent source to the local power path. If a power shelf,
upstream feed or AC source is interrupted, the BBU can sustain the rack while the fault is
isolated or another source assumes the load. A modular shelf further improves resilience

because one BBU failure need not remove the entire backup function; the remaining
modules can continue operating within the available redundancy and power limits.
Distributed deployment also narrows the fault domain. A defective rack BBU affects a
much smaller set of compute equipment than a failure within a centralized backup system
serving a large room or data hall. The tradeoff is a much larger population of batteries,
converters, controllers and fans that must be monitored, tested and eventually replaced.

Digital state-of-health monitoring, fault reporting and hot-swappable construction are
therefore central to the operating model rather than optional features.
BBUs vs. UPSs
While some of the functions that BBUs offer look similar to what traditional UPSs can do,
we think BBUs and UPSs should be viewed as complementary rather than substitutive
technologies, as they address different challenges within the datacenter power
architecture.
A UPS is designed to provide facility- or row-level protection against utility outages,

voltage sags, surges, and other power-quality disturbances, ensuring continuous AC power
delivery to a broad range of IT equipment. In contrast, a BBU operates much closer to the
AI workload, supplying DC power directly at the rack level and responding to localized
power events that occur faster than the upstream power infrastructure can efficiently
manage.
As AI racks become denser and more power intensive, the UPS continues to serve as the
first line of defense against external power disruptions, while the BBU acts as a second

layer of protection by stabilizing the rack DC bus, supporting transient load spikes, and
providing short-duration ride-through capability. Together, they create a hierarchical
power protection architecture in which the UPS ensures overall system availability and
power quality, while the BBU enhances rack-level resilience and power-delivery
performance. Rather than replacing the UPS, BBUs help reduce the burden on centralized
backup systems, improve power utilization efficiency, and enable the reliable operation of

next generation AI infrastructure with increasingly dynamic power profiles

### Page 34

M
Asia Pacific Insight
34

Capacitor backup unit (CBU) another complementary power

device
What's a CBU?
A CBU is an integrated energy storage subsystem positioned close to the AI load, typically
in a dedicated rack shelf, capacitor tray or adjacent power sidecar, and connected to the
server rack’s DC power path. Its principal storage medium may be an electric double-layer
capacitor (EDLC or supercapacitor), lithium-ion capacitor (LIC), or a bank of high-
capacitance electrolytic capacitors, depending on the required response time, energy

duration, footprint and cost. Dedicated CBU products increasingly use EDLCs or LICs
because their low internal resistance supports rapid, repetitive charge and discharge.
The CBU sits electrically alongside the PSU. Under steady-state conditions, the PSU
supplies the AI rack and maintains the CBU at its target state of charge. When GPU
demand temporarily exceeds the desired PSU output, the CBU discharges through a
controlled power converter into the DC bus; when GPU demand falls, the converter

reverses direction and recharges the capacitors at a controlled rate. This prevents the full
GPU load step from passing through the PSU and upstream equipment. Infineon notes
that a separate CBU must be compatible with, and potentially communicate with, the PSU
shelves to coordinate rack power.
An alternative to deploying a standalone CBU shelf is to integrate the energy-storage
capacitors directly into the power shelf, effectively combining power conversion and

transient-energy buffering within the same subsystem. NVIDIA’s GB300 NVL72
architecture is a good example: its enhanced power shelves incorporate a substantial
capacitor bank alongside the PSU modules, with NVIDIA allocating approximately 65
joules of stored energy per GPU.
NVIDIA reported that this approach reduced peak upstream power demand by roughly
30% in a measured Megatron LLM workload. Compared with a separate CBU, integrating
capacitors into the power shelf can save valuable rack space, shorten the electrical path

between the energy buffer and the load, and simplify coordination between the PSU and

### Page 35

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 35
energy-storage system, since charging, discharging, and PSU output can be managed as
one integrated power architecture. The trade-off is that capacitor capacity becomes more
tightly coupled to the power-shelf design, potentially reducing flexibility in independently
scaling or servicing the energy-storage system. Nevertheless, as AI racks become
increasingly power dense, integrating transient-energy storage directly into the power

shelf could become an attractive alternative to standalone CBUs, particularly where rack
space and fast transient response are key design priorities.
Exhibit 40: CBU
Source: Panasonic
Exhibit 41: GB300 power shelf with integrated capacitors
Source: Nvidia
Key components within a CBU
• Capacitor bank: The storage section contains many capacitor cells arranged in
series to reach the required voltage and in parallel to provide the required power
and energy. Individual EDLC cells commonly operate at only a few volts, so high-

voltage systems require long series strings; Abracon cites approximately 2.7 V as a
typical supercapacitor cell rating, while TI’s NVIDIA-oriented reference architecture
demonstrates an 800 V CBU using EDLC cells. LICs can provide more stored
energy than conventional EDLCs, while EDLCs generally emphasize lower
resistance, high peak power and very high cycling endurance. Delta, for example,
offers a power-capacitance shelf using fast-charge/discharge LICs, rated to provide

15 seconds of hold-up at 20 KW.
• Cell-balancing system: Because cell capacitance, leakage current and internal
resistance vary across a series string, the voltage does not divide perfectly equally.
A balancing circuit prevents an individual cell from exceeding its rated voltage
during charging or being reverse-biased during deep discharge. Passive balancing
uses resistors to bleed excess charge, while active approaches use MOSFETs,
amplifiers or switched converters to redistribute energy more efficiently; the

design tradeoff is between cost, balancing speed, component count and standby
loss.
• Bidirectional DC/DC converter: This is the active interface between the capacitor
bank and the rack DC bus. During a GPU power spike, it converts the capacitor’s
changing voltage into a regulated bus contribution; during lower demand, it
reverses power flow to recharge the bank. The converter also allows more of the
capacitors’ stored energy to be used than would be possible with a direct

connection, because capacitor voltage declines materially during discharge.

### Page 36

M
Asia Pacific Insight
36
Infineon specifically identifies a capacitor bank plus bidirectional converter as the
main CBU structure, while Skeleton Technologies notes that converter-based
integration provides greater energy utilization and more precise power-flow
control than a direct bus connection.
• Controller and management software: The controller determines when the CBU
should charge, remain idle or discharge, based on bus voltage, PSU output, rack

power limits and capacitor state. It may also calculate state of health, manage
balancing, regulate recharge current and coordinate multiple CBU modules.
• Protection and switching: A high-power capacitor bank can release very large
fault current, so fuses, contactors, pre-charge circuits and overvoltage, overcurrent
and overtemperature protection remain essential. Pre-charge limits inrush current
when the CBU is connected to the DC bus, while contactors or solid-state switches

isolate the bank during a fault or service event. Skeleton’s reference system
integrates switchgear, fuse-fault detection, pre-charge/discharge contactors and
protection against high-current and overheating events; industry discussions also
highlight the need for faster DC fault isolation as CBU voltage rises.
• Monitoring and communications: Voltage, current, temperature, equivalent series
resistance and state-of-health measurements allow operators to identify

degradation before the CBU can no longer meet its specified peak-power
requirement. Panasonic emphasizes voltage, temperature and ESR monitoring for
predictive maintenance, while Skeleton provides single-cell voltage sensing,
multiple temperature sensors and communication bus to its master controller.
• Thermal and mechanical system: Although supercapacitors are efficient, high
repetitive current produces resistive heating, particularly in cells, busbars,

converters and connectors. The shelf therefore requires designed airflow,
temperature sensing and, at higher power levels, potentially liquid cooling.
Skeleton’s cabinet uses controlled fan cooling and also offers liquid-cooled
configurations, while the Open Compute Project presentation from Panasonic
identifies lower-resistance capacitors and liquid cooling as development priorities
for future higher-output CBUs
Main functions of CBU
Transient power stabilization

One of the most fundamental roles of a CBU is to stabilize the rack DC power bus when
GPU power consumption changes very rapidly. AI workloads frequently alternate between
compute-intensive phases and lower-power communication, synchronization, or idle
phases. Because many GPUs operate in parallel, these transitions can create very large
changes in rack power within milliseconds.
When GPU demand suddenly increases, the PSU cannot instantaneously increase its

output. The CBU therefore discharges stored energy into the DC bus during this short
response gap, helping prevent voltage drop and excessive current excursions. Conversely,
when GPU demand suddenly falls, the CBU can absorb excess energy while the PSU
reduces its output, helping limit voltage overshoot.
The key objective here is power quality and system stability, rather than reducing the
overall power capacity required by the rack. In other words, the CBU acts as a fast

### Page 37

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 37
electrical shock absorber between the highly dynamic GPU load and the slower PSU. This
becomes increasingly important as AI racks move above 100 KW and synchronized GPU
workloads create larger absolute power swings.
Peak shaving
Another major function is peak shaving, whose objective is to limit the maximum power
that the PSU and upstream infrastructure need to supply.
The benefit is potentially significant because the PSU, connectors, protection devices, and

portions of the upstream power infrastructure no longer need to be oversized solely for
short-duration GPU peaks. It can also allow PSUs to operate closer to their optimal
loading range, improving utilization and potentially efficiency.
Short duration ride-through
The third function is ride-through, where the CBU temporarily powers the rack when the
normal power source is interrupted altogether. This is fundamentally different from the
first two functions because the issue is no longer a mismatch between GPU demand and

PSU response—the upstream power source itself has temporarily disappeared.
A sufficiently sized CBU can maintain the DC bus during events such as a brief AC
interruption, PSU switchover, source-transfer event, or temporary power-shelf failure.
Depending on the amount and type of capacitance installed, this support can range from
milliseconds to several seconds. For example, some LIC-based capacitance shelves are
designed to provide multi-second hold-up at high rack power.

However, ride-through is generally a secondary function for a CBU. Capacitors have high
power density but relatively low energy density, meaning they can deliver a large amount
of power very quickly but cannot economically sustain that power for long periods. As the
required backup duration increases from seconds toward minutes, a battery-based BBU
generally becomes more suitable.
Exhibit 42: Capacitors help smooth power delivery
Source: Lite On

### Page 38

M
Asia Pacific Insight
38
CBUs vs. BBUs
The two technologies should consequently be regarded as complementary rather than
directly substitutive. A CBU handles the high-power, short-duration and high-cycle portion
of the load profile, while a BBU supplies the larger amount of energy required when the
upstream source remains unavailable. Some describe BBUs and CBUs as coexisting in the
same power sidecar, and hybrid architectures can allocate fast transients to capacitors

while preserving battery charge and cycle life for longer outages.
The CBU’s major advantages are rapid bidirectional response, long cycle life, high
efficiency and the ability to absorb repeated GPU peaks without the same electrochemical
wear mechanism as a conventional battery. It can also recharge quickly between workload
cycles and may require less routine replacement. However, the CBU has materially lower
energy density, its voltage falls continuously during discharge, and large banks require

balancing, high-current protection, cooling and valuable rack or sidecar space.
Exhibit 43: Comparison of CBUs and BBUs
